========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because my regular doctor sent me, honestly. She says my weight has gotten too low and that people are worried, and now everybody’s sort of making a big fuss about it. I mean, I know I’m lighter than I used to be, but from my point of view it doesn’t feel like the emergency everyone else seems to think it is.\n\nI work in a shop, I’m a carpenter, so I’m on my feet and moving around a lot, and to me being light kind of feels like it helps. I don’t feel weighed down, if that makes sense. I can keep up with things better, move around the benches, climb, carry myself through the day without feeling slowed down. So I suppose I’m here because they’re concerned about how low my weight’s gotten… but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, because it sounds a bit unreasonable when it’s put like that. But yes, I do worry about it. If I eat anything that feels heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make around holidays, I start thinking about it almost immediately — like, that’s going to sit on me, that’s going to turn into weight, I’m going to lose the hold I’ve managed to keep on myself.\n\nIt’s not even just the food itself, it’s the feeling afterward. I get this sort of panic that I’ve let the line slip, and then I’ll have to work twice as hard to get back to where I feel steady again. I know other people might see it as just a meal or a treat, but in my head it feels like the beginning of losing control. So… yes, gaining weight frightens me, more than I probably admit to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I… honestly, I think people are overreacting. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’re expecting, but I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous.” My husband keeps saying I look too thin, and he’ll give me that worried look at the table or when I’m getting dressed, like he’s watching for something to prove his point. And the apprentices at the shop have started making little comments too — not cruel exactly, more awkward, like, “You’re disappearing,” or asking if I’m eating enough.\n\nBut from my side, I feel like they’re mistaking discipline for sickness. I’ve always been someone who works hard and keeps control of things. In carpentry, you measure, you trim, you take off what doesn’t need to be there — and I know that sounds like a strange comparison, but that’s how my mind goes. I feel like I’ve gotten myself to a place where I’m not sloppy with food, not just giving in to whatever’s put in front of me.\n\nSo, healthy… I suppose everyone else would say no. But I keep telling them I’m fine. I don’t feel like I’m seeing what they’re seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “good” is a hard word for me with that. Other people might think I’d look better with more weight on me — my husband would probably say that without hesitating — but when I picture it, I don’t feel relieved or prettier or healthier. I feel nervous.\n\nIf I imagine weighing even a bit more, my mind goes straight to, well, what did I eat to make that happen? Was it something heavy, something rich, something like the food we used to have at holidays? And then I start worrying right away that it’s going to keep going, that I’ll gain more and more, and I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. It doesn’t feel like just “a bit more” to me. It feels like the start of slipping.\n\nSo… no, I suppose I don’t really think I’d look good. Or maybe I can’t see it properly. I just know the thought of it makes me feel like I’d be losing control of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape that you're trying to maintain?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a particular number, exactly… or at least I don’t say to myself, “I must stay at this weight.” It’s more like there’s a shape, or a feeling of being contained, that I’m trying not to lose. I know that probably sounds vague, but it’s very real in my head.\n\nIf I eat something heavy, or let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make around holidays — the rich things, the things everyone else treats like comfort — I start worrying almost immediately. Not later, not when my clothes fit differently, but right away. I think, that’s going to become weight, that’s going to change me, and then I’ll have lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I suppose what I’m trying to maintain is… control. A body that doesn’t feel like it’s getting away from me. I don’t have a neat answer like a dress size or a goal weight, but I’m afraid of crossing some line where I can’t pull it back again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about yourself if you had a more muscular or athletic build?\n\nPatient: More muscular or athletic… I’m not sure I think about it that way, honestly. I know people say “strong” like it’s obviously a good thing, and in my work I’ve always respected strength — you have to, doing carpentry. There’s a kind of usefulness to muscle, something practical. But when I imagine my own body changing like that, even if someone called it athletic, my mind doesn’t really stop at “strong” or “healthy.”\n\nIt goes to weight. It goes to bulk. It goes to, what would I have to eat for that to happen? And then I start getting anxious in that same old way. If I eat anything heavy, or let myself have those foods we used to make for holidays — the rich things, the filling things — I start worrying almost immediately that it’s going to turn into weight on me. Like it won’t just be strength, it’ll be me losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I suppose… no, I don’t think I’d feel better about myself just because it was called muscular or athletic. Maybe other people would see it differently, but to me it still feels like gaining, and gaining feels frightening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a particular part of your body where you are most afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s one particular part, like my stomach or my hips or something I could point to neatly. It’s more… the whole idea of weight appearing on me anywhere feels threatening. I know that sounds evasive, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nIf I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for holidays — rich food, filling food, the sort everyone else seems to enjoy without thinking — I start worrying right away. Almost immediately. Not because I can see a change in one spot, but because my mind says, this is going to turn into weight, and then I won’t be able to stop it.\n\nSo it’s not so much that I’m watching one body part more than all the others. It’s the fear that any gaining means I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. Once that thought starts, it’s very hard to calm it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you think you look?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, but not in a simple way like, “I don’t like how I look in the mirror.” It’s more tangled than that. It’s like how I imagine I’ll look if I let go even a little. If I eat something heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays — the rich, filling kinds of things — I start worrying right away that it’s going to show on me somehow. That I’ll gain weight, and then I won’t look like myself anymore, or I won’t feel like I have control over myself.\n\nAnd I know one meal shouldn’t feel like it has that much power, but in my head it does. It feels immediate, like the moment I’ve eaten it I’ve set something in motion. Then I start thinking I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. So yes, I suppose it is related to how I think I look, but it’s also about being afraid that my body will change and I won’t be able to pull it back.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t always like saying it that plainly, because part of me wants to make it sound more practical, like I’m just busy or focused on work. And on workshop days that’s what I tell myself — that I’ve got boards laid out, measurements in my head, tools going, and stopping for a real meal would interrupt the rhythm.\n\nBut honestly, it’s also because eating more feels risky. Most workshop days I’ll just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel like I have to. Nothing that feels like a “meal,” really. A real meal feels unnecessary in the moment, and it throws off this sense of control I’ve got over the day. Once I stop and eat properly, I start thinking about whether it’s too much, whether it’ll sit on me, whether I’ve lost hold of things.\n\nSo yes, I limit it. I dress it up as being busy, but the fear of gaining weight is there underneath it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often skip meals because you're worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I don’t always call it “skipping meals” in my own head, because that makes it sound more deliberate than I want to admit. I tell myself I’m just in the middle of something, especially on workshop days. If I’m working, I’ll have tea and keep going, and maybe later I’ll take a few careful bites of something, just enough to feel like I’ve had something, but not enough that it turns into a real meal.\n\nA proper meal feels like… it interrupts everything. Not just the work, though that’s part of it. It interrupts the control I feel like I have over the day. Once I stop and eat properly, then I start worrying about what it means, whether it was too much, whether I should have waited, whether it’s going to change something. So I usually avoid that whole spiral by not stopping in the first place.\n\nSo yes, I skip meals, especially when I’m worried that eating more will lead to gaining weight. It feels safer to keep it small and controlled.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say it hasn’t, because I still go to the shop, I still show up, I still try to act like everything is running the way it always has. But it’s changed things.\n\nMy husband notices more than I want him to. He’ll hover a bit, or ask if I’m all right, or step in before I’ve asked for help. And the apprentices have started doing that too — taking over some of the heavier lifting, moving boards, handling things I used to just do myself without anyone thinking twice about it. They try to make it seem casual, like they’re just being useful, but I can tell they’re checking on me.\n\nAnd I know they probably mean well. That’s the difficult part. I’m not angry in a clean way, because I know it comes from concern. But it makes the whole shop feel different. Less like mine. Less like a place where I’m just the one in charge, working alongside everyone, and more like a place where people are watching me to see if I can manage.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my relationships. Not always through arguments or anything dramatic, but through this constant… adjustment around me. People taking over, asking careful questions, not quite trusting me to do what I’ve always done. And that hurts more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens or something you think about when you start to feel anxious about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually it’s tied to what I think of as “heavy” food. I don’t even know if that’s the right word, but that’s how it feels in my head. If something seems rich, or substantial, or like it would really sit in me, I start worrying almost right away.\n\nAnd holidays are especially difficult, because there are foods we used to make without thinking so much about it. Things that were part of the day, part of family, part of just… being together. But now if I let myself have those, even a little more than I planned, my mind goes straight to weight. I start thinking, What if this is what makes me gain? What if I lose the grip I’ve been trying so hard to keep?\n\nIt’s not even only after I eat it. Sometimes it starts before, just looking at it or smelling it, knowing what it is. I can feel myself calculating, bargaining, trying to decide whether I can manage a bite and still feel in control. And if I do eat it, I don’t really enjoy it the way I used to, because the worry comes in so quickly. It feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve opened a door I shouldn’t have opened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is tied to how you think your body would look if you were heavier?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because then it sounds shallow, or vain, and I don’t think that’s exactly it. It’s more like… if I imagine being heavier, I imagine feeling like I’ve lost control of my own body. Like it would show on me somehow, and everyone would be able to see that I’d let things slip.\n\nWhen I eat something I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the holiday foods we used to make without all this fuss in my head, the worry comes on almost immediately. It’s not just “that was too much” in some abstract way. It turns into, What will this do to me? Will I look different? Will my clothes feel different? Will I have lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep?\n\nAnd I know one meal shouldn’t have that much meaning. Part of me knows that. But in the moment it feels like it does. It feels like if I allow those foods, especially the richer ones, then I’m opening the door to gaining weight, and then my body won’t feel like mine in the same way. So yes, I suppose the fear is tied to how I think I’d look if I were heavier… but also to what that would mean to me. It would feel like failure, like losing control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to a fear of not being able to fit into your current clothes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s specifically about fitting into my current clothes… not in a simple way, anyway. I mean, clothes are part of it in the background, I suppose, because clothes tell you things, don’t they? If something feels tighter, it’s hard not to take that as proof that something has gone wrong.\n\nBut what comes first for me is usually the food itself. If I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays — the things that used to just be normal, part of the day — I start worrying almost immediately that I’m going to gain weight. It’s like my mind jumps straight there before I’ve even had time to enjoy anything.\n\nAnd then it becomes this feeling that I’ve lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s really the thing I’m afraid of, I think. Not just one waistband or one shirt, but the sense that if I allow too much, or eat the wrong thing, then I won’t be able to hold the line anymore. So yes, clothes can get tangled up in it, but the fear is bigger than that. It’s the gaining itself, and what it would mean about me not being in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to not wanting to have a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t know that I have one exact “shape” in mind, like I could point to a picture and say, that’s what I’m afraid of. It’s more this fear that if I gain weight, my body will start to feel unfamiliar to me, or like it’s changing in a way I can’t pull back from.\n\nWhen I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying right away. It’s almost automatic. I’ll think, this is going to make me gain, this is how it starts, I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. And then I imagine it showing somehow, even if I know that sounds unreasonable from the outside.\n\nIt’s not just wanting to look a certain way, exactly. It’s more that the thought of looking different feels like proof that I’ve failed or let go too much. Like my body would be announcing something I’m trying so hard to control. So yes, the appearance part is in there, but for me it’s tied very tightly to the fear of gaining weight and not being able to hold that line anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I know that probably sounds vague, but it’s very real in my head.\n\nIf I eat something heavy, or let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make around holidays — the rich things, the things everyone else treats like comfort — I start worrying almost immediately. Not later, not when my clothes fit differently, but right away. I think, that’s going to become weight, that’s going to change me, and then I’ll have lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I suppose what I’m trying to maintain is… control. A body that doesn’t feel like it’s getting away from me. I don’t have a neat answer like a dress size or a goal weight, but I’m afraid of crossing some line where I can’t pull it back again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about yourself if you had a more muscular or athletic build?\n\nPatient: More muscular or athletic… I’m not sure I think about it that way, honestly. I know people say “strong” like it’s obviously a good thing, and in my work I’ve always respected strength — you have to, doing carpentry. There’s a kind of usefulness to muscle, something practical. But when I imagine my own body changing like that, even if someone called it athletic, my mind doesn’t really stop at “strong” or “healthy.”\n\nIt goes to weight. It goes to bulk. It goes to, what would I have to eat for that to happen? And then I start getting anxious in that same old way. If I eat anything heavy, or let myself have those foods we used to make for holidays — the rich things, the filling things — I start worrying almost immediately that it’s going to turn into weight on me. Like it won’t just be strength, it’ll be me losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I suppose… no, I don’t think I’d feel better about myself just because it was called muscular or athletic. Maybe other people would see it differently, but to me it still feels like gaining, and gaining feels frightening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a particular part of your body where you are most afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s one particular part, like my stomach or my hips or something I could point to neatly. It’s more… the whole idea of weight appearing on me anywhere feels threatening. I know that sounds evasive, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nIf I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for holidays — rich food, filling food, the sort everyone else seems to enjoy without thinking — I start worrying right away. Almost immediately. Not because I can see a change in one spot, but because my mind says, this is going to turn into weight, and then I won’t be able to stop it.\n\nSo it’s not so much that I’m watching one body part more than all the others. It’s the fear that any gaining means I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. Once that thought starts, it’s very hard to calm it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you think you look?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, but not in a simple way like, “I don’t like how I look in the mirror.” It’s more tangled than that. It’s like how I imagine I’ll look if I let go even a little. If I eat something heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays — the rich, filling kinds of things — I start worrying right away that it’s going to show on me somehow. That I’ll gain weight, and then I won’t look like myself anymore, or I won’t feel like I have control over myself.\n\nAnd I know one meal shouldn’t feel like it has that much power, but in my head it does. It feels immediate, like the moment I’ve eaten it I’ve set something in motion. Then I start thinking I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. So yes, I suppose it is related to how I think I look, but it’s also about being afraid that my body will change and I won’t be able to pull it back.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t always like saying it that plainly, because part of me wants to make it sound more practical, like I’m just busy or focused on work. And on workshop days that’s what I tell myself — that I’ve got boards laid out, measurements in my head, tools going, and stopping for a real meal would interrupt the rhythm.\n\nBut honestly, it’s also because eating more feels risky. Most workshop days I’ll just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel like I have to. Nothing that feels like a “meal,” really. A real meal feels unnecessary in the moment, and it throws off this sense of control I’ve got over the day. Once I stop and eat properly, I start thinking about whether it’s too much, whether it’ll sit on me, whether I’ve lost hold of things.\n\nSo yes, I limit it. I dress it up as being busy, but the fear of gaining weight is there underneath it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often skip meals because you're worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I don’t always call it “skipping meals” in my own head, because that makes it sound more deliberate than I want to admit. I tell myself I’m just in the middle of something, especially on workshop days. If I’m working, I’ll have tea and keep going, and maybe later I’ll take a few careful bites of something, just enough to feel like I’ve had something, but not enough that it turns into a real meal.\n\nA proper meal feels like… it interrupts everything. Not just the work, though that’s part of it. It interrupts the control I feel like I have over the day. Once I stop and eat properly, then I start worrying about what it means, whether it was too much, whether I should have waited, whether it’s going to change something. So I usually avoid that whole spiral by not stopping in the first place.\n\nSo yes, I skip meals, especially when I’m worried that eating more will lead to gaining weight. It feels safer to keep it small and controlled.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say it hasn’t, because I still go to the shop, I still show up, I still try to act like everything is running the way it always has. But it’s changed things.\n\nMy husband notices more than I want him to. He’ll hover a bit, or ask if I’m all right, or step in before I’ve asked for help. And the apprentices have started doing that too — taking over some of the heavier lifting, moving boards, handling things I used to just do myself without anyone thinking twice about it. They try to make it seem casual, like they’re just being useful, but I can tell they’re checking on me.\n\nAnd I know they probably mean well. That’s the difficult part. I’m not angry in a clean way, because I know it comes from concern. But it makes the whole shop feel different. Less like mine. Less like a place where I’m just the one in charge, working alongside everyone, and more like a place where people are watching me to see if I can manage.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my relationships. Not always through arguments or anything dramatic, but through this constant… adjustment around me. People taking over, asking careful questions, not quite trusting me to do what I’ve always done. And that hurts more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens or something you think about when you start to feel anxious about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually it’s tied to what I think of as “heavy” food. I don’t even know if that’s the right word, but that’s how it feels in my head. If something seems rich, or substantial, or like it would really sit in me, I start worrying almost right away.\n\nAnd holidays are especially difficult, because there are foods we used to make without thinking so much about it. Things that were part of the day, part of family, part of just… being together. But now if I let myself have those, even a little more than I planned, my mind goes straight to weight. I start thinking, What if this is what makes me gain? What if I lose the grip I’ve been trying so hard to keep?\n\nIt’s not even only after I eat it. Sometimes it starts before, just looking at it or smelling it, knowing what it is. I can feel myself calculating, bargaining, trying to decide whether I can manage a bite and still feel in control. And if I do eat it, I don’t really enjoy it the way I used to, because the worry comes in so quickly. It feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve opened a door I shouldn’t have opened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is tied to how you think your body would look if you were heavier?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because then it sounds shallow, or vain, and I don’t think that’s exactly it. It’s more like… if I imagine being heavier, I imagine feeling like I’ve lost control of my own body. Like it would show on me somehow, and everyone would be able to see that I’d let things slip.\n\nWhen I eat something I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the holiday foods we used to make without all this fuss in my head, the worry comes on almost immediately. It’s not just “that was too much” in some abstract way. It turns into, What will this do to me? Will I look different? Will my clothes feel different? Will I have lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep?\n\nAnd I know one meal shouldn’t have that much meaning. Part of me knows that. But in the moment it feels like it does. It feels like if I allow those foods, especially the richer ones, then I’m opening the door to gaining weight, and then my body won’t feel like mine in the same way. So yes, I suppose the fear is tied to how I think I’d look if I were heavier… but also to what that would mean to me. It would feel like failure, like losing control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to a fear of not being able to fit into your current clothes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s specifically about fitting into my current clothes… not in a simple way, anyway. I mean, clothes are part of it in the background, I suppose, because clothes tell you things, don’t they? If something feels tighter, it’s hard not to take that as proof that something has gone wrong.\n\nBut what comes first for me is usually the food itself. If I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays — the things that used to just be normal, part of the day — I start worrying almost immediately that I’m going to gain weight. It’s like my mind jumps straight there before I’ve even had time to enjoy anything.\n\nAnd then it becomes this feeling that I’ve lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s really the thing I’m afraid of, I think. Not just one waistband or one shirt, but the sense that if I allow too much, or eat the wrong thing, then I won’t be able to hold the line anymore. So yes, clothes can get tangled up in it, but the fear is bigger than that. It’s the gaining itself, and what it would mean about me not being in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to not wanting to have a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t know that I have one exact “shape” in mind, like I could point to a picture and say, that’s what I’m afraid of. It’s more this fear that if I gain weight, my body will start to feel unfamiliar to me, or like it’s changing in a way I can’t pull back from.\n\nWhen I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying right away. It’s almost automatic. I’ll think, this is going to make me gain, this is how it starts, I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. And then I imagine it showing somehow, even if I know that sounds unreasonable from the outside.\n\nIt’s not just wanting to look a certain way, exactly. It’s more that the thought of looking different feels like proof that I’ve failed or let go too much. Like my body would be announcing something I’m trying so hard to control. So yes, the appearance part is in there, but for me it’s tied very tightly to the fear of gaining weight and not being able to hold that line anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that there’s one specific size or shape I’m trying to avoid, like a number or a particular body type I could name. It’s more… any sign that I’m getting bigger feels frightening to me. Even saying that makes me uncomfortable, because it sounds so blunt, but that’s what happens in my head.

If I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. Before I’ve even really finished, sometimes. I’ll think, What if this makes me gain weight? What if this is the thing that starts it? And then it becomes less about a specific shape and more about feeling like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.

So I guess I’m not avoiding one exact body shape as much as I’m trying to avoid that feeling of change, of gaining, of my body moving away from what I can control. That’s the part that scares me.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because my regular doctor sent me, honestly. She says my weight has gotten too low and that people are worried, and now everybody’s sort of making a big fuss about it. I mean, I know I’m lighter than I used to be, but from my point of view it doesn’t feel like the emergency everyone else seems to think it is.\n\nI work in a shop, I’m a carpenter, so I’m on my feet and moving around a lot, and to me being light kind of feels like it helps. I don’t feel weighed down, if that makes sense. I can keep up with things better, move around the benches, climb, carry myself through the day without feeling slowed down. So I suppose I’m here because they’re concerned about how low my weight’s gotten… but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, because it sounds a bit unreasonable when it’s put like that. But yes, I do worry about it. If I eat anything that feels heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make around holidays, I start thinking about it almost immediately — like, that’s going to sit on me, that’s going to turn into weight, I’m going to lose the hold I’ve managed to keep on myself.\n\nIt’s not even just the food itself, it’s the feeling afterward. I get this sort of panic that I’ve let the line slip, and then I’ll have to work twice as hard to get back to where I feel steady again. I know other people might see it as just a meal or a treat, but in my head it feels like the beginning of losing control. So… yes, gaining weight frightens me, more than I probably admit to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I… honestly, I think people are overreacting. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’re expecting, but I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous.” My husband keeps saying I look too thin, and he’ll give me that worried look at the table or when I’m getting dressed, like he’s watching for something to prove his point. And the apprentices at the shop have started making little comments too — not cruel exactly, more awkward, like, “You’re disappearing,” or asking if I’m eating enough.\n\nBut from my side, I feel like they’re mistaking discipline for sickness. I’ve always been someone who works hard and keeps control of things. In carpentry, you measure, you trim, you take off what doesn’t need to be there — and I know that sounds like a strange comparison, but that’s how my mind goes. I feel like I’ve gotten myself to a place where I’m not sloppy with food, not just giving in to whatever’s put in front of me.\n\nSo, healthy… I suppose everyone else would say no. But I keep telling them I’m fine. I don’t feel like I’m seeing what they’re seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “good” is a hard word for me with that. Other people might think I’d look better with more weight on me — my husband would probably say that without hesitating — but when I picture it, I don’t feel relieved or prettier or healthier. I feel nervous.\n\nIf I imagine weighing even a bit more, my mind goes straight to, well, what did I eat to make that happen? Was it something heavy, something rich, something like the food we used to have at holidays? And then I start worrying right away that it’s going to keep going, that I’ll gain more and more, and I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. It doesn’t feel like just “a bit more” to me. It feels like the start of slipping.\n\nSo… no, I suppose I don’t really think I’d look good. Or maybe I can’t see it properly. I just know the thought of it makes me feel like I’d be losing control of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape that you're trying to maintain?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a particular number, exactly… or at least I don’t say to myself, “I must stay at this weight.” It’s more like there’s a shape, or a feeling of being contained, that I’m trying not to lose. I know that probably sounds vague, but it’s very real in my head.\n\nIf I eat something heavy, or let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make around holidays — the rich things, the things everyone else treats like comfort — I start worrying almost immediately. Not later, not when my clothes fit differently, but right away. I think, that’s going to become weight, that’s going to change me, and then I’ll have lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I suppose what I’m trying to maintain is… control. A body that doesn’t feel like it’s getting away from me. I don’t have a neat answer like a dress size or a goal weight, but I’m afraid of crossing some line where I can’t pull it back again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about yourself if you had a more muscular or athletic build?\n\nPatient: More muscular or athletic… I’m not sure I think about it that way, honestly. I know people say “strong” like it’s obviously a good thing, and in my work I’ve always respected strength — you have to, doing carpentry. There’s a kind of usefulness to muscle, something practical. But when I imagine my own body changing like that, even if someone called it athletic, my mind doesn’t really stop at “strong” or “healthy.”\n\nIt goes to weight. It goes to bulk. It goes to, what would I have to eat for that to happen? And then I start getting anxious in that same old way. If I eat anything heavy, or let myself have those foods we used to make for holidays — the rich things, the filling things — I start worrying almost immediately that it’s going to turn into weight on me. Like it won’t just be strength, it’ll be me losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I suppose… no, I don’t think I’d feel better about myself just because it was called muscular or athletic. Maybe other people would see it differently, but to me it still feels like gaining, and gaining feels frightening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a particular part of your body where you are most afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s one particular part, like my stomach or my hips or something I could point to neatly. It’s more… the whole idea of weight appearing on me anywhere feels threatening. I know that sounds evasive, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nIf I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for holidays — rich food, filling food, the sort everyone else seems to enjoy without thinking — I start worrying right away. Almost immediately. Not because I can see a change in one spot, but because my mind says, this is going to turn into weight, and then I won’t be able to stop it.\n\nSo it’s not so much that I’m watching one body part more than all the others. It’s the fear that any gaining means I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. Once that thought starts, it’s very hard to calm it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you think you look?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, but not in a simple way like, “I don’t like how I look in the mirror.” It’s more tangled than that. It’s like how I imagine I’ll look if I let go even a little. If I eat something heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays — the rich, filling kinds of things — I start worrying right away that it’s going to show on me somehow. That I’ll gain weight, and then I won’t look like myself anymore, or I won’t feel like I have control over myself.\n\nAnd I know one meal shouldn’t feel like it has that much power, but in my head it does. It feels immediate, like the moment I’ve eaten it I’ve set something in motion. Then I start thinking I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. So yes, I suppose it is related to how I think I look, but it’s also about being afraid that my body will change and I won’t be able to pull it back.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t always like saying it that plainly, because part of me wants to make it sound more practical, like I’m just busy or focused on work. And on workshop days that’s what I tell myself — that I’ve got boards laid out, measurements in my head, tools going, and stopping for a real meal would interrupt the rhythm.\n\nBut honestly, it’s also because eating more feels risky. Most workshop days I’ll just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel like I have to. Nothing that feels like a “meal,” really. A real meal feels unnecessary in the moment, and it throws off this sense of control I’ve got over the day. Once I stop and eat properly, I start thinking about whether it’s too much, whether it’ll sit on me, whether I’ve lost hold of things.\n\nSo yes, I limit it. I dress it up as being busy, but the fear of gaining weight is there underneath it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often skip meals because you're worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I don’t always call it “skipping meals” in my own head, because that makes it sound more deliberate than I want to admit. I tell myself I’m just in the middle of something, especially on workshop days. If I’m working, I’ll have tea and keep going, and maybe later I’ll take a few careful bites of something, just enough to feel like I’ve had something, but not enough that it turns into a real meal.\n\nA proper meal feels like… it interrupts everything. Not just the work, though that’s part of it. It interrupts the control I feel like I have over the day. Once I stop and eat properly, then I start worrying about what it means, whether it was too much, whether I should have waited, whether it’s going to change something. So I usually avoid that whole spiral by not stopping in the first place.\n\nSo yes, I skip meals, especially when I’m worried that eating more will lead to gaining weight. It feels safer to keep it small and controlled.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say it hasn’t, because I still go to the shop, I still show up, I still try to act like everything is running the way it always has. But it’s changed things.\n\nMy husband notices more than I want him to. He’ll hover a bit, or ask if I’m all right, or step in before I’ve asked for help. And the apprentices have started doing that too — taking over some of the heavier lifting, moving boards, handling things I used to just do myself without anyone thinking twice about it. They try to make it seem casual, like they’re just being useful, but I can tell they’re checking on me.\n\nAnd I know they probably mean well. That’s the difficult part. I’m not angry in a clean way, because I know it comes from concern. But it makes the whole shop feel different. Less like mine. Less like a place where I’m just the one in charge, working alongside everyone, and more like a place where people are watching me to see if I can manage.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my relationships. Not always through arguments or anything dramatic, but through this constant… adjustment around me. People taking over, asking careful questions, not quite trusting me to do what I’ve always done. And that hurts more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens or something you think about when you start to feel anxious about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually it’s tied to what I think of as “heavy” food. I don’t even know if that’s the right word, but that’s how it feels in my head. If something seems rich, or substantial, or like it would really sit in me, I start worrying almost right away.\n\nAnd holidays are especially difficult, because there are foods we used to make without thinking so much about it. Things that were part of the day, part of family, part of just… being together. But now if I let myself have those, even a little more than I planned, my mind goes straight to weight. I start thinking, What if this is what makes me gain? What if I lose the grip I’ve been trying so hard to keep?\n\nIt’s not even only after I eat it. Sometimes it starts before, just looking at it or smelling it, knowing what it is. I can feel myself calculating, bargaining, trying to decide whether I can manage a bite and still feel in control. And if I do eat it, I don’t really enjoy it the way I used to, because the worry comes in so quickly. It feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve opened a door I shouldn’t have opened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is tied to how you think your body would look if you were heavier?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because then it sounds shallow, or vain, and I don’t think that’s exactly it. It’s more like… if I imagine being heavier, I imagine feeling like I’ve lost control of my own body. Like it would show on me somehow, and everyone would be able to see that I’d let things slip.\n\nWhen I eat something I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the holiday foods we used to make without all this fuss in my head, the worry comes on almost immediately. It’s not just “that was too much” in some abstract way. It turns into, What will this do to me? Will I look different? Will my clothes feel different? Will I have lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep?\n\nAnd I know one meal shouldn’t have that much meaning. Part of me knows that. But in the moment it feels like it does. It feels like if I allow those foods, especially the richer ones, then I’m opening the door to gaining weight, and then my body won’t feel like mine in the same way. So yes, I suppose the fear is tied to how I think I’d look if I were heavier… but also to what that would mean to me. It would feel like failure, like losing control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to a fear of not being able to fit into your current clothes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s specifically about fitting into my current clothes… not in a simple way, anyway. I mean, clothes are part of it in the background, I suppose, because clothes tell you things, don’t they? If something feels tighter, it’s hard not to take that as proof that something has gone wrong.\n\nBut what comes first for me is usually the food itself. If I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays — the things that used to just be normal, part of the day — I start worrying almost immediately that I’m going to gain weight. It’s like my mind jumps straight there before I’ve even had time to enjoy anything.\n\nAnd then it becomes this feeling that I’ve lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s really the thing I’m afraid of, I think. Not just one waistband or one shirt, but the sense that if I allow too much, or eat the wrong thing, then I won’t be able to hold the line anymore. So yes, clothes can get tangled up in it, but the fear is bigger than that. It’s the gaining itself, and what it would mean about me not being in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to not wanting to have a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t know that I have one exact “shape” in mind, like I could point to a picture and say, that’s what I’m afraid of. It’s more this fear that if I gain weight, my body will start to feel unfamiliar to me, or like it’s changing in a way I can’t pull back from.\n\nWhen I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying right away. It’s almost automatic. I’ll think, this is going to make me gain, this is how it starts, I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. And then I imagine it showing somehow, even if I know that sounds unreasonable from the outside.\n\nIt’s not just wanting to look a certain way, exactly. It’s more that the thought of looking different feels like proof that I’ve failed or let go too much. Like my body would be announcing something I’m trying so hard to control. So yes, the appearance part is in there, but for me it’s tied very tightly to the fear of gaining weight and not being able to hold that line anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific body shape or size that you are trying to avoid by not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s one specific size or shape I’m trying to avoid, like a number or a particular body type I could name. It’s more… any sign that I’m getting bigger feels frightening to me. Even saying that makes me uncomfortable, because it sounds so blunt, but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nIf I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. Before I’ve even really finished, sometimes. I’ll think, What if this makes me gain weight? What if this is the thing that starts it? And then it becomes less about a specific shape and more about feeling like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I guess I’m not avoiding one exact body shape as much as I’m trying to avoid that feeling of change, of gaining, of my body moving away from what I can control. That’s the part that scares me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It feels like if I allow those foods, especially the richer ones, then I’m opening the door to gaining weight, and then my body won’t feel like mine in the same way. So yes, I suppose the fear is tied to how I think I’d look if I were heavier… but also to what that would mean to me. It would feel like failure, like losing control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to a fear of not being able to fit into your current clothes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s specifically about fitting into my current clothes… not in a simple way, anyway. I mean, clothes are part of it in the background, I suppose, because clothes tell you things, don’t they? If something feels tighter, it’s hard not to take that as proof that something has gone wrong.\n\nBut what comes first for me is usually the food itself. If I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays — the things that used to just be normal, part of the day — I start worrying almost immediately that I’m going to gain weight. It’s like my mind jumps straight there before I’ve even had time to enjoy anything.\n\nAnd then it becomes this feeling that I’ve lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s really the thing I’m afraid of, I think. Not just one waistband or one shirt, but the sense that if I allow too much, or eat the wrong thing, then I won’t be able to hold the line anymore. So yes, clothes can get tangled up in it, but the fear is bigger than that. It’s the gaining itself, and what it would mean about me not being in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to not wanting to have a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t know that I have one exact “shape” in mind, like I could point to a picture and say, that’s what I’m afraid of. It’s more this fear that if I gain weight, my body will start to feel unfamiliar to me, or like it’s changing in a way I can’t pull back from.\n\nWhen I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying right away. It’s almost automatic. I’ll think, this is going to make me gain, this is how it starts, I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. And then I imagine it showing somehow, even if I know that sounds unreasonable from the outside.\n\nIt’s not just wanting to look a certain way, exactly. It’s more that the thought of looking different feels like proof that I’ve failed or let go too much. Like my body would be announcing something I’m trying so hard to control. So yes, the appearance part is in there, but for me it’s tied very tightly to the fear of gaining weight and not being able to hold that line anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific body shape or size that you are trying to avoid by not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s one specific size or shape I’m trying to avoid, like a number or a particular body type I could name. It’s more… any sign that I’m getting bigger feels frightening to me. Even saying that makes me uncomfortable, because it sounds so blunt, but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nIf I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. And then it becomes less about a specific shape and more about feeling like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I guess I’m not avoiding one exact body shape as much as I’m trying to avoid that feeling of change, of gaining, of my body moving away from what I can control. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure it’s an “ideal” in the way people usually mean that, like some picture I’m trying to match or some perfect shape I have in my head. I don’t really think of it that clearly. It’s more like… I’m trying to keep from crossing a line, even if I couldn’t tell you exactly where that line is.

What happens is, if I eat something I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have foods we used to make around the holidays, the worry starts almost immediately. I’ll think, Oh no, this is going to make me gain weight. And then it’s not just about that meal anymore. It turns into this whole feeling that I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.

So maybe the “ideal” is just staying where I feel I can still control it. Not getting bigger, not feeling like my body is moving away from me. I know that probably isn’t a real ideal exactly, but it feels like something I have to maintain, because once I imagine gaining, I feel like I won’t be able to stop it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because my regular doctor sent me, honestly. She says my weight has gotten too low and that people are worried, and now everybody’s sort of making a big fuss about it. I mean, I know I’m lighter than I used to be, but from my point of view it doesn’t feel like the emergency everyone else seems to think it is.\n\nI work in a shop, I’m a carpenter, so I’m on my feet and moving around a lot, and to me being light kind of feels like it helps. I don’t feel weighed down, if that makes sense. I can keep up with things better, move around the benches, climb, carry myself through the day without feeling slowed down. So I suppose I’m here because they’re concerned about how low my weight’s gotten… but I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, because it sounds a bit unreasonable when it’s put like that. But yes, I do worry about it. If I eat anything that feels heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make around holidays, I start thinking about it almost immediately — like, that’s going to sit on me, that’s going to turn into weight, I’m going to lose the hold I’ve managed to keep on myself.\n\nIt’s not even just the food itself, it’s the feeling afterward. I get this sort of panic that I’ve let the line slip, and then I’ll have to work twice as hard to get back to where I feel steady again. I know other people might see it as just a meal or a treat, but in my head it feels like the beginning of losing control. So… yes, gaining weight frightens me, more than I probably admit to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level?\n\nPatient: I… honestly, I think people are overreacting. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’re expecting, but I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous.” My husband keeps saying I look too thin, and he’ll give me that worried look at the table or when I’m getting dressed, like he’s watching for something to prove his point. And the apprentices at the shop have started making little comments too — not cruel exactly, more awkward, like, “You’re disappearing,” or asking if I’m eating enough.\n\nBut from my side, I feel like they’re mistaking discipline for sickness. I’ve always been someone who works hard and keeps control of things. In carpentry, you measure, you trim, you take off what doesn’t need to be there — and I know that sounds like a strange comparison, but that’s how my mind goes. I feel like I’ve gotten myself to a place where I’m not sloppy with food, not just giving in to whatever’s put in front of me.\n\nSo, healthy… I suppose everyone else would say no. But I keep telling them I’m fine. I don’t feel like I’m seeing what they’re seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look good if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “good” is a hard word for me with that. Other people might think I’d look better with more weight on me — my husband would probably say that without hesitating — but when I picture it, I don’t feel relieved or prettier or healthier. I feel nervous.\n\nIf I imagine weighing even a bit more, my mind goes straight to, well, what did I eat to make that happen? Was it something heavy, something rich, something like the food we used to have at holidays? And then I start worrying right away that it’s going to keep going, that I’ll gain more and more, and I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. It doesn’t feel like just “a bit more” to me. It feels like the start of slipping.\n\nSo… no, I suppose I don’t really think I’d look good. Or maybe I can’t see it properly. I just know the thought of it makes me feel like I’d be losing control of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape that you're trying to maintain?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s a particular number, exactly… or at least I don’t say to myself, “I must stay at this weight.” It’s more like there’s a shape, or a feeling of being contained, that I’m trying not to lose. I know that probably sounds vague, but it’s very real in my head.\n\nIf I eat something heavy, or let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make around holidays — the rich things, the things everyone else treats like comfort — I start worrying almost immediately. Not later, not when my clothes fit differently, but right away. I think, that’s going to become weight, that’s going to change me, and then I’ll have lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I suppose what I’m trying to maintain is… control. A body that doesn’t feel like it’s getting away from me. I don’t have a neat answer like a dress size or a goal weight, but I’m afraid of crossing some line where I can’t pull it back again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about yourself if you had a more muscular or athletic build?\n\nPatient: More muscular or athletic… I’m not sure I think about it that way, honestly. I know people say “strong” like it’s obviously a good thing, and in my work I’ve always respected strength — you have to, doing carpentry. There’s a kind of usefulness to muscle, something practical. But when I imagine my own body changing like that, even if someone called it athletic, my mind doesn’t really stop at “strong” or “healthy.”\n\nIt goes to weight. It goes to bulk. It goes to, what would I have to eat for that to happen? And then I start getting anxious in that same old way. If I eat anything heavy, or let myself have those foods we used to make for holidays — the rich things, the filling things — I start worrying almost immediately that it’s going to turn into weight on me. Like it won’t just be strength, it’ll be me losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I suppose… no, I don’t think I’d feel better about myself just because it was called muscular or athletic. Maybe other people would see it differently, but to me it still feels like gaining, and gaining feels frightening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a particular part of your body where you are most afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s one particular part, like my stomach or my hips or something I could point to neatly. It’s more… the whole idea of weight appearing on me anywhere feels threatening. I know that sounds evasive, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nIf I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for holidays — rich food, filling food, the sort everyone else seems to enjoy without thinking — I start worrying right away. Almost immediately. Not because I can see a change in one spot, but because my mind says, this is going to turn into weight, and then I won’t be able to stop it.\n\nSo it’s not so much that I’m watching one body part more than all the others. It’s the fear that any gaining means I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. Once that thought starts, it’s very hard to calm it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to how you think you look?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, but not in a simple way like, “I don’t like how I look in the mirror.” It’s more tangled than that. It’s like how I imagine I’ll look if I let go even a little. If I eat something heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays — the rich, filling kinds of things — I start worrying right away that it’s going to show on me somehow. That I’ll gain weight, and then I won’t look like myself anymore, or I won’t feel like I have control over myself.\n\nAnd I know one meal shouldn’t feel like it has that much power, but in my head it does. It feels immediate, like the moment I’ve eaten it I’ve set something in motion. Then I start thinking I’ll lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. So yes, I suppose it is related to how I think I look, but it’s also about being afraid that my body will change and I won’t be able to pull it back.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t always like saying it that plainly, because part of me wants to make it sound more practical, like I’m just busy or focused on work. And on workshop days that’s what I tell myself — that I’ve got boards laid out, measurements in my head, tools going, and stopping for a real meal would interrupt the rhythm.\n\nBut honestly, it’s also because eating more feels risky. Most workshop days I’ll just have tea, and maybe a few careful bites of something if I feel like I have to. Nothing that feels like a “meal,” really. A real meal feels unnecessary in the moment, and it throws off this sense of control I’ve got over the day. Once I stop and eat properly, I start thinking about whether it’s too much, whether it’ll sit on me, whether I’ve lost hold of things.\n\nSo yes, I limit it. I dress it up as being busy, but the fear of gaining weight is there underneath it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often skip meals because you're worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I don’t always call it “skipping meals” in my own head, because that makes it sound more deliberate than I want to admit. I tell myself I’m just in the middle of something, especially on workshop days. If I’m working, I’ll have tea and keep going, and maybe later I’ll take a few careful bites of something, just enough to feel like I’ve had something, but not enough that it turns into a real meal.\n\nA proper meal feels like… it interrupts everything. Not just the work, though that’s part of it. It interrupts the control I feel like I have over the day. Once I stop and eat properly, then I start worrying about what it means, whether it was too much, whether I should have waited, whether it’s going to change something. So I usually avoid that whole spiral by not stopping in the first place.\n\nSo yes, I skip meals, especially when I’m worried that eating more will lead to gaining weight. It feels safer to keep it small and controlled.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say it hasn’t, because I still go to the shop, I still show up, I still try to act like everything is running the way it always has. But it’s changed things.\n\nMy husband notices more than I want him to. He’ll hover a bit, or ask if I’m all right, or step in before I’ve asked for help. And the apprentices have started doing that too — taking over some of the heavier lifting, moving boards, handling things I used to just do myself without anyone thinking twice about it. They try to make it seem casual, like they’re just being useful, but I can tell they’re checking on me.\n\nAnd I know they probably mean well. That’s the difficult part. I’m not angry in a clean way, because I know it comes from concern. But it makes the whole shop feel different. Less like mine. Less like a place where I’m just the one in charge, working alongside everyone, and more like a place where people are watching me to see if I can manage.\n\nSo yes, it’s affected my relationships. Not always through arguments or anything dramatic, but through this constant… adjustment around me. People taking over, asking careful questions, not quite trusting me to do what I’ve always done. And that hurts more than I like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens or something you think about when you start to feel anxious about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually it’s tied to what I think of as “heavy” food. I don’t even know if that’s the right word, but that’s how it feels in my head. If something seems rich, or substantial, or like it would really sit in me, I start worrying almost right away.\n\nAnd holidays are especially difficult, because there are foods we used to make without thinking so much about it. Things that were part of the day, part of family, part of just… being together. But now if I let myself have those, even a little more than I planned, my mind goes straight to weight. I start thinking, What if this is what makes me gain? What if I lose the grip I’ve been trying so hard to keep?\n\nIt’s not even only after I eat it. Sometimes it starts before, just looking at it or smelling it, knowing what it is. I can feel myself calculating, bargaining, trying to decide whether I can manage a bite and still feel in control. And if I do eat it, I don’t really enjoy it the way I used to, because the worry comes in so quickly. It feels like I’ve let something slip, like I’ve opened a door I shouldn’t have opened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is tied to how you think your body would look if you were heavier?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because then it sounds shallow, or vain, and I don’t think that’s exactly it. It’s more like… if I imagine being heavier, I imagine feeling like I’ve lost control of my own body. Like it would show on me somehow, and everyone would be able to see that I’d let things slip.\n\nWhen I eat something I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the holiday foods we used to make without all this fuss in my head, the worry comes on almost immediately. It’s not just “that was too much” in some abstract way. It turns into, What will this do to me? Will I look different? Will my clothes feel different? Will I have lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep?\n\nAnd I know one meal shouldn’t have that much meaning. Part of me knows that. But in the moment it feels like it does. It feels like if I allow those foods, especially the richer ones, then I’m opening the door to gaining weight, and then my body won’t feel like mine in the same way. So yes, I suppose the fear is tied to how I think I’d look if I were heavier… but also to what that would mean to me. It would feel like failure, like losing control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to a fear of not being able to fit into your current clothes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s specifically about fitting into my current clothes… not in a simple way, anyway. I mean, clothes are part of it in the background, I suppose, because clothes tell you things, don’t they? If something feels tighter, it’s hard not to take that as proof that something has gone wrong.\n\nBut what comes first for me is usually the food itself. If I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays — the things that used to just be normal, part of the day — I start worrying almost immediately that I’m going to gain weight. It’s like my mind jumps straight there before I’ve even had time to enjoy anything.\n\nAnd then it becomes this feeling that I’ve lost the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. That’s really the thing I’m afraid of, I think. Not just one waistband or one shirt, but the sense that if I allow too much, or eat the wrong thing, then I won’t be able to hold the line anymore. So yes, clothes can get tangled up in it, but the fear is bigger than that. It’s the gaining itself, and what it would mean about me not being in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is related to not wanting to have a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t know that I have one exact “shape” in mind, like I could point to a picture and say, that’s what I’m afraid of. It’s more this fear that if I gain weight, my body will start to feel unfamiliar to me, or like it’s changing in a way I can’t pull back from.\n\nWhen I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying right away. It’s almost automatic. I’ll think, this is going to make me gain, this is how it starts, I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. And then I imagine it showing somehow, even if I know that sounds unreasonable from the outside.\n\nIt’s not just wanting to look a certain way, exactly. It’s more that the thought of looking different feels like proof that I’ve failed or let go too much. Like my body would be announcing something I’m trying so hard to control. So yes, the appearance part is in there, but for me it’s tied very tightly to the fear of gaining weight and not being able to hold that line anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific body shape or size that you are trying to avoid by not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s one specific size or shape I’m trying to avoid, like a number or a particular body type I could name. It’s more… any sign that I’m getting bigger feels frightening to me. Even saying that makes me uncomfortable, because it sounds so blunt, but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nIf I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. Before I’ve even really finished, sometimes. I’ll think, What if this makes me gain weight? What if this is the thing that starts it? And then it becomes less about a specific shape and more about feeling like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I guess I’m not avoiding one exact body shape as much as I’m trying to avoid that feeling of change, of gaining, of my body moving away from what I can control. That’s the part that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a particular body ideal that you are trying to maintain by not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s an “ideal” in the way people usually mean that, like some picture I’m trying to match or some perfect shape I have in my head. I don’t really think of it that clearly. It’s more like… I’m trying to keep from crossing a line, even if I couldn’t tell you exactly where that line is.\n\nWhat happens is, if I eat something I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have foods we used to make around the holidays, the worry starts almost immediately. I’ll think, Oh no, this is going to make me gain weight. And then it’s not just about that meal anymore. It turns into this whole feeling that I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo maybe the “ideal” is just staying where I feel I can still control it. Not getting bigger, not feeling like my body is moving away from me. I know that probably isn’t a real ideal exactly, but it feels like something I have to maintain, because once I imagine gaining, I feel like I won’t be able to stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

think it is, at least partly. I don’t know that I have one exact “shape” in mind, like I could point to a picture and say, that’s what I’m afraid of. It’s more this fear that if I gain weight, my body will start to feel unfamiliar to me, or like it’s changing in a way I can’t pull back from.\n\nWhen I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make around holidays, I start worrying right away. It’s almost automatic. I’ll think, this is going to make me gain, this is how it starts, I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. So yes, the appearance part is in there, but for me it’s tied very tightly to the fear of gaining weight and not being able to hold that line anymore.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific body shape or size that you are trying to avoid by not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s one specific size or shape I’m trying to avoid, like a number or a particular body type I could name. It’s more… any sign that I’m getting bigger feels frightening to me. Even saying that makes me uncomfortable, because it sounds so blunt, but that’s what happens in my head.\n\nIf I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. Before I’ve even really finished, sometimes. I’ll think, What if this makes me gain weight? What if this is the thing that starts it? And then it becomes less about a specific shape and more about feeling like I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo I guess I’m not avoiding one exact body shape as much as I’m trying to avoid that feeling of change, of gaining, of my body moving away from what I can control. That’s the part that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a particular body ideal that you are trying to maintain by not gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s an “ideal” in the way people usually mean that, like some picture I’m trying to match or some perfect shape I have in my head. I don’t really think of it that clearly. It’s more like… I’m trying to keep from crossing a line, even if I couldn’t tell you exactly where that line is.\n\nWhat happens is, if I eat something I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have foods we used to make around the holidays, the worry starts almost immediately. I’ll think, Oh no, this is going to make me gain weight. And then it’s not just about that meal anymore. It turns into this whole feeling that I’m losing the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\n\nSo maybe the “ideal” is just staying where I feel I can still control it. Not getting bigger, not feeling like my body is moving away from me. I know that probably isn’t a real ideal exactly, but it feels like something I have to maintain, because once I imagine gaining, I feel like I won’t be able to stop it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas something specific happened in the past that made you start worrying about gaining weight?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure there was one single thing, like one comment or one event I can point to and say, that’s when it started. It feels more tangled than that. But I do know the worry really attaches itself to certain foods and situations.

Like if I eat anything I think of as heavy, or if I let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying almost right away. It’s not even after I’ve seen any change or anything like that. It’s immediate. I’ll be sitting there thinking, this is going to make me gain weight, and then the panic kind of builds around that thought.

And it’s not just the food itself. It’s what it represents, I guess. Those holiday foods especially feel tied to letting go, eating more freely, not keeping track in the same way. Then I start thinking I’m going to lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. So I don’t know if something specific “happened” in the past, exactly, but those kinds of moments are definitely where the fear comes up strongest.
